<a id='question-049'></a>

### Question 049: SRY Deletion on Y Chromosome (46,XY Female Swyer Variant)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The SRY gene on the Y chromosome is crucial for testis determination in males. Its absence or mutation leads to gonadal dysgenesis, resulting in a 46,XY karyotype but female phenotype due to the lack of testicular development and subsequent androgen/AMH production. This affects the development of internal and external genitalia. (SRY基因位于Y染色体上，对雄性睾丸决定至关重要。其缺失或突变导致性腺发育不良，导致46,XY核型但由于缺乏睾丸发育和随后的雄激素/AMH产生而呈现女性表型。这会影响内外生殖器的发育。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 17-year-old phenotypic female presents to the gynecology clinic due to primary amenorrhea. She has normal female secondary sexual characteristics (breast development, pubic hair distribution) but reports no menarche. Her past medical history is unremarkable, and she denies any significant illnesses or surgeries. Physical examination reveals normal external female genitalia, normal breast development (Tanner stage V), and sparse pubic and axillary hair (Tanner stage II). Pelvic examination shows a blind-ending vaginal pouch and absence of a uterus and ovaries. Karyotype analysis reveals a 46,XY chromosomal constitution. Serum levels of follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH) and luteinizing hormone (LH) are markedly elevated, while testosterone and estrogen levels are low. An abdominal ultrasound confirms the absence of intra-abdominal gonads.

Which of the following structures is most likely absent in this patient due to the underlying genetic defect?

(A) Müllerian ducts
(B) Wolffian ducts
(C) Urogenital sinus
(D) Paramesonephric ducts
(E) Sertoli cells

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 17-year-old phenotypic female with primary amenorrhea. This immediately points towards issues with female reproductive system development or function.
- **Phenotype:** Normal female secondary sexual characteristics (breasts) but sparse pubic/axillary hair. Breast development suggests some estrogen exposure (possibly peripheral conversion or residual ovarian tissue, though ultrasound denies intra-abdominal gonads), while sparse pubic/axillary hair suggests lack of significant androgen exposure.
- **Physical Exam:** Normal external female genitalia, blind-ending vagina, absent uterus/ovaries. This indicates failure of internal female reproductive organ development.
- **Karyotype:** 46,XY. This is the crucial clue. A genetically male individual (XY) has developed a female phenotype.
- **Hormone Levels:** High FSH/LH, low testosterone/estrogen. High gonadotropins indicate primary gonadal failure (gonads are not responding to pituitary signals). Low sex steroids confirm lack of functional gonads.
- **Ultrasound:** Absence of intra-abdominal gonads. Confirms gonadal dysgenesis.
- **Diagnosis:** The combination of 46,XY karyotype, female phenotype, primary amenorrhea, absent uterus/ovaries, high FSH/LH, and absent gonads points strongly to Swyer syndrome (46,XY gonadal dysgenesis), typically caused by mutations or deletions in the SRY gene.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which structure is most likely absent. In Swyer syndrome, the SRY gene is non-functional, preventing testis development. Without testes, testosterone and Anti-Müllerian Hormone (AMH) are not produced.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **SRY Gene Function:** The SRY (Sex-determining Region Y) gene, located on the Y chromosome, encodes the testis-determining factor (TDF). TDF initiates the cascade of events leading to the differentiation of the bipotential gonad into a testis during early embryonic development (around week 6-7).
- **Testis Development:** If SRY is functional, the bipotential gonad develops into a testis. Testes produce testosterone and AMH.
- **Role of Testosterone:** Testosterone is essential for the development of the Wolffian ducts into male internal genitalia (epididymis, vas deferens, seminal vesicles, ejaculatory ducts). Testosterone is also converted to dihydrotestosterone (DHT) by 5α-reductase, which is crucial for the development of male external genitalia (penis, scrotum) from the genital tubercle, urethral folds, and labioscrotal swellings.
- **Role of AMH:** AMH (also known as Müllerian-inhibiting substance or MIS) causes the regression of the Müllerian ducts, which would otherwise develop into the female internal genitalia (fallopian tubes, uterus, upper vagina).
- **Swyer Syndrome (46,XY Gonadal Dysgenesis):** In the absence of a functional SRY gene (due to deletion, mutation, or translocation), the bipotential gonad develops into a streak gonad, which is non-functional.
- **Consequences of Non-functional SRY:**
    - No testis formation.
    - No testosterone production.
    - No AMH production.
    - Absence of testosterone leads to the failure of Wolffian duct development.
    - Absence of AMH allows the Müllerian ducts to develop into female internal genitalia (uterus, fallopian tubes, upper vagina).
    - Absence of testosterone and DHT leads to the development of female external genitalia.
- **Patient's Presentation:** The patient has a 46,XY karyotype but a female phenotype. This means the SRY gene is non-functional, leading to streak gonads. The absence of AMH allowed the Müllerian ducts to develop (though the uterus is absent, which is a common finding in Swyer syndrome, possibly due to other genetic factors or incomplete development). The absence of testosterone prevented Wolffian duct development. The absence of testosterone/DHT led to female external genitalia. The question asks which structure is *absent*. Since testosterone is absent, the Wolffian ducts fail to develop.

- **Distractor Analysis:**
    - (A) Müllerian ducts: These ducts develop into female internal genitalia (fallopian tubes, uterus, upper vagina). In the absence of AMH (due to non-functional testes), they *develop*, not regress. While the uterus is absent in this specific patient, the *potential* for Müllerian duct development exists, and they are not inherently absent due to the SRY defect itself.
    - (C) Urogenital sinus: This structure gives rise to the lower vagina, urethra, and bladder. Its development is generally unaffected by the SRY gene status.
    - (D) Paramesonephric ducts: This is another name for the Müllerian ducts. See analysis for (A).
    - (E) Sertoli cells: These cells are located within the seminiferous tubules of the testes and are essential for spermatogenesis. Since testes do not develop in Swyer syndrome, Sertoli cells are absent. However, the question asks for a structure that is *absent* due to the *underlying genetic defect* affecting the *developmental pathway*. The Wolffian ducts are the primary structures whose development is directly inhibited by the lack of testosterone produced by non-existent testes. While Sertoli cells are also absent, the Wolffian ducts represent the direct consequence of the lack of testicular androgen production on internal genital development. The absence of Wolffian ducts is a defining feature of the lack of testicular development.

- **Conclusion:** The absence of functional testes due to the SRY deletion leads to a lack of testosterone. Testosterone is required for the development of the Wolffian ducts. Therefore, the Wolffian ducts are absent in this patient.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Müllerian ducts: Incorrect. The Müllerian ducts develop into the female internal reproductive organs (fallopian tubes, uterus, upper vagina). In the absence of functional testes, Anti-Müllerian Hormone (AMH) is not produced, leading to the *persistence* and development of the Müllerian ducts, not their absence.
- (C) Urogenital sinus: Incorrect. The urogenital sinus contributes to the formation of the lower vagina, urethra, and bladder. Its development is largely independent of the SRY gene and testicular function.
- (D) Paramesonephric ducts: Incorrect. This is another name for the Müllerian ducts. As explained for option (A), these ducts develop in the absence of functional testes.
- (E) Sertoli cells: Incorrect. Sertoli cells are located within the testes and are essential for sperm development. Since testes do not develop in Swyer syndrome due to the SRY deletion, Sertoli cells are indeed absent. However, the Wolffian ducts represent the direct consequence of the lack of testosterone production, which is the primary hormonal defect stemming from the non-functional SRY gene regarding the development of internal genitalia. The absence of Wolffian ducts is a more direct and defining consequence of the lack of testicular androgen production compared to the absence of Sertoli cells (which are part of the non-developed testis itself). The question focuses on the structures affected by the *lack of testicular function*, and the Wolffian ducts are the key structures whose development is dependent on testicular androgen production.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure           | Development Trigger | Presence/Absence in 46,XY Swyer Syndrome | Role                                                                 |
| :------------------ | :------------------ | :--------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Wolffian ducts      | Testosterone        | Absent                                   | Develop into epididymis, vas deferens, seminal vesicles, ejaculatory ducts |
| Müllerian ducts     | Absence of AMH      | Present (though uterus may be absent)     | Develop into fallopian tubes, uterus, upper vagina                   |
| Testes              | Functional SRY      | Absent (Streak Gonads)                   | Produce testosterone and AMH                                         |
| Sertoli cells       | Functional SRY      | Absent                                   | Support spermatogenesis within testes                                 |
| External Genitalia | Testosterone/DHT    | Female                                   | Develop based on androgen levels                                     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The SRY gene on the Y chromosome is essential for testis development. Its absence or mutation leads to gonadal dysgenesis (Swyer syndrome) in 46,XY individuals. Lack of testes results in absent testosterone and AMH production. Absence of testosterone causes regression of the Wolffian ducts. Absence of AMH allows development of the Müllerian ducts. Key takeaway: SRY -> Testis -> Testosterone (Wolffian duct development) & AMH (Müllerian duct regression). Differential: Turner syndrome (45,X) involves streak gonads but different karyotype and mechanism. Androgen insensitivity syndrome (AIS) involves functional testes but androgen receptor defect. Congenital adrenal hyperplasia (CAH) involves adrenal enzyme defects affecting steroidogenesis.

<a id='question-050'></a>

### Question 050: Epigenetic Imprinting in Early Germline Development
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Genomic imprinting is an epigenetic phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. This process is established during gametogenesis and involves differential methylation patterns. Errors in imprinting can lead to developmental disorders. (基因组印记是一种表观遗传现象，某些基因的表达方式取决于其亲本来源。这个过程在配子发生过程中建立，涉及不同的甲基化模式。印记错误会导致发育障碍。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old primigravida presents for routine prenatal screening at 16 weeks gestation. Family history is significant for her father having a history of overgrowth syndrome characterized by macroglossia, hemihypertrophy, and an increased risk of certain childhood tumors. Ultrasound reveals macrosomia (estimated fetal weight >95th percentile for gestational age), macroglossia, and an omphalocele. Karyotype analysis of fetal cells obtained via amniocentesis shows a normal 46,XX karyotype. Further molecular genetic testing reveals uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 11p15.5, specifically maternal UPD, meaning both copies of chromosome 11p15.5 are inherited from the mother. The father is confirmed to carry a heterozygous deletion of the paternal allele at the same locus. Given the father's history and the fetal findings, which of the following mechanisms best explains the observed phenotype in the fetus?

(A) Loss of maternal imprinting control region (ICR) methylation, leading to biallelic expression of paternally expressed genes.
(B) Gain of paternal imprinting control region (ICR) methylation, leading to silencing of maternally expressed genes.
(C) Loss of paternal imprinting control region (ICR) methylation, leading to biallelic expression of maternally expressed genes.
(D) Gain of maternal imprinting control region (ICR) methylation, leading to silencing of paternally expressed genes.
(E) Complete deletion of the maternal allele at the 11p15.5 locus, resulting in haploinsufficiency.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 16-week fetus with macrosomia, macroglossia, and omphalocele. These are classic features of Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome (BWS).
- **Family History:** Father has overgrowth syndrome (suggesting a genetic component, possibly related to imprinting).
- **Genetic Findings:** Fetal karyotype is normal (46,XX), ruling out chromosomal aneuploidy. Molecular testing shows maternal UPD for chromosome 11p15.5. The father has a heterozygous deletion of the paternal allele at 11p15.5.
- **Connecting Clues:** BWS is often associated with abnormalities at the 11p15.5 locus, which contains imprinted genes. Maternal UPD 11p15.5 means the fetus has two maternal copies and no paternal copy. The father's deletion confirms the paternal allele is missing. The phenotype (overgrowth) suggests issues with genes normally expressed from the paternal allele at this locus.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **11p15.5 Locus:** This region contains two major imprinted gene clusters: IGF2 (Insulin-like Growth Factor 2) and H19.
- **Imprinting Mechanism:** The Imprinting Control Region (ICR) on chromosome 11p15.5 dictates the parent-of-origin-specific expression. In normal development, the paternal allele at the ICR is methylated, which silences the nearby H19 gene and allows for expression of the IGF2 gene. The maternal allele at the ICR is unmethylated, which silences the IGF2 gene and allows for expression of the H19 gene.
- **Maternal UPD 11p15.5:** In the fetus, both copies of chromosome 11p15.5 are maternal. Therefore, both copies of the ICR are unmethylated. This leads to silencing of IGF2 (as the maternal ICR silences it) and expression of H19 from both copies.
- **Consequence:** Loss of paternal IGF2 expression (a growth promoter) and gain of maternal H19 expression (a growth suppressor, although its role is complex) leads to the overgrowth phenotype characteristic of BWS.
- **Father's Deletion:** The father's heterozygous deletion of the paternal allele at 11p15.5 explains his history of overgrowth syndrome, likely due to loss of paternal IGF2 expression.
- **Option Analysis:**
    - (A) Loss of maternal ICR methylation would lead to silencing of H19 and expression of IGF2 from the maternal allele, which is the normal state. This doesn't explain the phenotype.
    - (B) Gain of paternal ICR methylation would silence H19 and allow IGF2 expression from the paternal allele, which is also the normal state. This doesn't explain the phenotype.
    - (C) Loss of paternal ICR methylation (as occurs with maternal UPD) leads to silencing of IGF2 and expression of H19 from both maternal copies. This correctly explains the loss of paternal IGF2 function and the resulting overgrowth.
    - (D) Gain of maternal ICR methylation would lead to silencing of H19 and expression of IGF2 from the maternal allele. This is not the mechanism in maternal UPD.
    - (E) While the father has a deletion, the fetus has UPD. Haploinsufficiency of a single allele isn't the primary mechanism in UPD, where the *pattern* of expression (parent-of-origin) is disrupted.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Loss of maternal imprinting control region (ICR) methylation would result in the *normal* expression pattern where the maternal allele silences IGF2 and expresses H19, and the paternal allele expresses IGF2 and silences H19. This is incorrect because the fetus has maternal UPD 11p15.5, leading to two maternal copies.
- (B): Gain of paternal imprinting control region (ICR) methylation would also result in the *normal* expression pattern, where the paternal allele expresses IGF2 and the maternal allele expresses H19. This is incorrect for the same reason as (A).
- (D): Gain of maternal imprinting control region (ICR) methylation would lead to silencing of H19 and expression of IGF2 from the maternal allele. This is not the mechanism in maternal UPD 11p15.5, where the maternal ICR is *unmethylated*.
- (E): Complete deletion of the maternal allele at the 11p15.5 locus is incorrect. The fetus has maternal UPD, meaning it has two maternal copies and zero paternal copies. Haploinsufficiency refers to a condition where having only one functional copy of a gene is insufficient, which is not the primary issue here; the issue is the *pattern* of expression due to imprinting disruption.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Normal Imprinting (11p15.5) | Maternal UPD 11p15.5 | Paternal Deletion 11p15.5 |
|----------------------|-----------------------------|-----------------------|---------------------------|
| ICR Methylation (Paternal) | Methylated                  | Unmethylated          | N/A (Deleted)             |
| ICR Methylation (Maternal) | Unmethylated                | Unmethylated          | N/A                      |
| IGF2 Expression      | Paternal allele only        | None (Silenced)       | Reduced/Absent           |
| H19 Expression       | Maternal allele only        | Both alleles         | Normal                   |
| Phenotype            | Normal                      | Overgrowth (BWS)      | Overgrowth (BWS)          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Genomic imprinting involves parent-of-origin-specific gene expression regulated by methylation patterns at Imprinting Control Regions (ICRs). Maternal UPD 11p15.5 leads to loss of paternal IGF2 expression and gain of maternal H19 expression, causing Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome. Key differentials include Prader-Willi syndrome (loss of paternal 15q11-q13) and Angelman syndrome (loss of maternal 15q11-q13), which also involve imprinting but at different loci and with different phenotypes.
